I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day in some dramatic way, but more days than not, yes, the thought has been there.

It’s often not loud, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. I don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.

After a while I just pack it all away again. It’s not that I hate it now. It’s more that the pleasure seems to have gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worry or anxiety over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean… I do worry, of course, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things over too much, so it’s hard for me to know where ordinary worry ends and something else begins.\n\nOver these months, I suppose there have been moments when my mind gets caught on things and turns them over and over, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a separate feeling of being anxious in the way people describe it—like panic, or being keyed up all the time. It’s more… I don’t know. More of a heaviness than a nervousness, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say worry has been the main thing I’ve noticed. At least, not in a way I can clearly separate out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not very much, no. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to sound as though everything is completely flat, but… the things I used to look forward to don’t seem to pull me toward them the way they did.\n\nThe clearest one is still the watercolor painting. I’ll sometimes get everything out—the paints, the brushes, the little jar of water, the paper taped down just so—and there’s a part of me that remembers liking all of it. I remember how I used to enjoy noticing the color in a flower, or the way light sat on the edge of a table. But now I’ll sit there looking at the paper, and I’m almost waiting for that feeling to arrive, and it just… doesn’t. After a while it starts to feel a bit silly, sitting there like that, so I pack it all away again.\n\nSo I suppose I still try to do the activities, or at least I circle around them, but I can’t honestly say I look forward to them or get much pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasurable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, even a little bit, but it’s hard to find an honest example. The closest thing is still my watercolors, because I do sometimes set everything out as though I’m going to paint. I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I look at the paper, and I look at the colors, and I can remember that I used to love that—the way one color would soften into another, or the way light would catch on something ordinary. I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually part of what scared me enough to come in.\n\nIt isn’t that I have a plan to hurt myself, because I don’t. I want to be clear about that. But I’ve found myself having thoughts like, “It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow,” or that if something happened naturally, I wouldn’t have to keep trying so hard. And then I get frightened by the fact that I’m thinking that way at all.\n\nThey’re not constant every minute, but they’ve been coming up enough that I can’t just brush them off as a passing bad mood. It feels more like these quiet thoughts in the background, especially when I’m worn down. I haven’t made any plan, and I haven’t taken steps to do anything to myself, but the thoughts are there, and that’s not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow more days than not over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day in some dramatic way, but more days than not, yes, the thought has been there.\n\nIt’s often not loud, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. That’s the part that really scared me enough to come in, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say something like that.\n\nI don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually part of what scared me enough to come in.\n\nIt isn’t that I have a plan to hurt myself, because I don’t. I want to be clear about that. But I’ve found myself having thoughts like, “It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow,” or that if something happened naturally, I wouldn’t have to keep trying so hard. And then I get frightened by the fact that I’m thinking that way at all.\n\nThey’re not constant every minute, but they’ve been coming up enough that I can’t just brush them off as a passing bad mood. It feels more like these quiet thoughts in the background, especially when I’m worn down. I haven’t made any plan, and I haven’t taken steps to do anything to myself, but the thoughts are there, and that’s not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow more days than not over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day in some dramatic way, but more days than not, yes, the thought has been there.\n\nIt’s often not loud, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. That’s the part that really scared me enough to come in, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say something like that.\n\nI don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling this way, with this grayness and lack of pleasure, for more than two years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about eight months, since my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not more than two years. It feels long, and I suppose in the middle of it eight months can feel like forever, but it hasn’t been years.

It really started about eight months ago, around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That was when I began noticing this grayness settling in, and things I used to enjoy just didn’t feel the same. Before that, I had difficult days, of course, but not this same ongoing lack of pleasure. This has been more like a stretch of months since all of that happened with him.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I’ve been having these thoughts that frightened me. Not exactly that I have some plan to hurt myself—I don’t—but more like… I’ll be lying there and think, “It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow.” And then I feel ashamed even saying that out loud, because part of me thinks, well, that sounds dramatic, and I don’t mean to be dramatic. But it’s been coming up enough that it scared me. I thought maybe I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside or pretending it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: About eight months, I think. It wasn’t all at once, exactly—it sort of started after my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That really shook me, more than I think I understood at first. Since then, those thoughts have just… appeared now and then, especially when I’m alone with it. So yes, roughly eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or empty, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days, really, it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That sounds a bit poetic, maybe, but it’s the closest way I can describe it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, like making a cup of tea, or standing at the window looking out at my garden, and it’s not that anything terrible is happening in that moment, exactly. It’s just… there’s this quiet heaviness sitting in me.\n\nAnd I try to talk myself out of it sometimes. I’ll think, “Well, the garden is lovely,” or “You have no reason to feel this way right now,” but reasoning with it doesn’t seem to lift it. It’s just there, most days, like the color has been turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, unfortunately. I used to paint a bit—watercolors, mostly. Nothing grand, just small things, flowers from the garden or the way the light fell across the table in the afternoon. It used to give me this very quiet pleasure, choosing the colors, watching them bleed into each other on the paper.\n\nAnd I still get the things out sometimes. I’ll lay out the paints and fill the little jar with water and tape down the paper, almost as if going through the motions might remind me how it used to feel. But then I just sit there looking at the blank page. I can see that there’s color in front of me, and light, and all the things I used to love noticing… but the feeling doesn’t come. After a while I just pack it all away again. It’s not that I hate it now. It’s more that the pleasure seems to have gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worry or anxiety over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean… I do worry, of course, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things over too much, so it’s hard for me to know where ordinary worry ends and something else begins.\n\nOver these months, I suppose there have been moments when my mind gets caught on things and turns them over and over, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a separate feeling of being anxious in the way people describe it—like panic, or being keyed up all the time. It’s more… I don’t know. More of a heaviness than a nervousness, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say worry has been the main thing I’ve noticed. At least, not in a way I can clearly separate out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not very much, no. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to sound as though everything is completely flat, but… the things I used to look forward to don’t seem to pull me toward them the way they did.\n\nThe clearest one is still the watercolor painting. I’ll sometimes get everything out—the paints, the brushes, the little jar of water, the paper taped down just so—and there’s a part of me that remembers liking all of it. I remember how I used to enjoy noticing the color in a flower, or the way light sat on the edge of a table. But now I’ll sit there looking at the paper, and I’m almost waiting for that feeling to arrive, and it just… doesn’t. After a while it starts to feel a bit silly, sitting there like that, so I pack it all away again.\n\nSo I suppose I still try to do the activities, or at least I circle around them, but I can’t honestly say I look forward to them or get much pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasurable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, even a little bit, but it’s hard to find an honest example. The closest thing is still my watercolors, because I do sometimes set everything out as though I’m going to paint. I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I look at the paper, and I look at the colors, and I can remember that I used to love that—the way one color would soften into another, or the way light would catch on something ordinary. I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually part of what scared me enough to come in.\n\nIt isn’t that I have a plan to hurt myself, because I don’t. I want to be clear about that. It feels more like these quiet thoughts in the background, especially when I’m worn down. I haven’t made any plan, and I haven’t taken steps to do anything to myself, but the thoughts are there, and that’s not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow more days than not over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Yes… I think I have. Maybe not every single thing, but the things that used to feel like mine, like they gave me a little bit of quiet joy, don’t really do that now.

Watercolor is the clearest example. I still set my things out sometimes — the paper, the brushes, the little pans of color — almost like if I go through the motions, maybe the feeling will come back. I’ll sit there looking at the blank paper, and I can remember that I used to feel something when I saw the colors and the light, especially the way water would move the pigment around. It used to be calming, almost absorbing.

But now I just sit there. I stare at it, and nothing really arrives. No spark, no pleasure in it. After a while I end up putting it all away again, and that feels awful in its own quiet way, because it’s not that I don’t know how to do it anymore. It’s just that the part of me that used to enjoy it feels sort of… unreachable.

I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself moving faster, no — I don’t think I’ve been pacing or feeling physically wound up like that.

So yes, roughly eight months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or empty, over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. Most days, really, it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That sounds a bit poetic, maybe, but it’s the closest way I can describe it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, like making a cup of tea, or standing at the window looking out at my garden, and it’s not that anything terrible is happening in that moment, exactly. It’s just… there’s this quiet heaviness sitting in me.\n\nAnd I try to talk myself out of it sometimes. I’ll think, “Well, the garden is lovely,” or “You have no reason to feel this way right now,” but reasoning with it doesn’t seem to lift it. It’s just there, most days, like the color has been turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, unfortunately. I used to paint a bit—watercolors, mostly. Nothing grand, just small things, flowers from the garden or the way the light fell across the table in the afternoon. It used to give me this very quiet pleasure, choosing the colors, watching them bleed into each other on the paper.\n\nAnd I still get the things out sometimes. I’ll lay out the paints and fill the little jar with water and tape down the paper, almost as if going through the motions might remind me how it used to feel. But then I just sit there looking at the blank page. I can see that there’s color in front of me, and light, and all the things I used to love noticing… but the feeling doesn’t come. After a while I just pack it all away again. It’s not that I hate it now. It’s more that the pleasure seems to have gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worry or anxiety over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean… I do worry, of course, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things over too much, so it’s hard for me to know where ordinary worry ends and something else begins.\n\nOver these months, I suppose there have been moments when my mind gets caught on things and turns them over and over, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a separate feeling of being anxious in the way people describe it—like panic, or being keyed up all the time. It’s more… I don’t know. More of a heaviness than a nervousness, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say worry has been the main thing I’ve noticed. At least, not in a way I can clearly separate out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not very much, no. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to sound as though everything is completely flat, but… the things I used to look forward to don’t seem to pull me toward them the way they did.\n\nThe clearest one is still the watercolor painting. I’ll sometimes get everything out—the paints, the brushes, the little jar of water, the paper taped down just so—and there’s a part of me that remembers liking all of it. I remember how I used to enjoy noticing the color in a flower, or the way light sat on the edge of a table. But now I’ll sit there looking at the paper, and I’m almost waiting for that feeling to arrive, and it just… doesn’t. After a while it starts to feel a bit silly, sitting there like that, so I pack it all away again.\n\nSo I suppose I still try to do the activities, or at least I circle around them, but I can’t honestly say I look forward to them or get much pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasurable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, even a little bit, but it’s hard to find an honest example. The closest thing is still my watercolors, because I do sometimes set everything out as though I’m going to paint. I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I look at the paper, and I look at the colors, and I can remember that I used to love that—the way one color would soften into another, or the way light would catch on something ordinary. I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually part of what scared me enough to come in.\n\nIt isn’t that I have a plan to hurt myself, because I don’t. I want to be clear about that. But I’ve found myself having thoughts like, “It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow,” or that if something happened naturally, I wouldn’t have to keep trying so hard. And then I get frightened by the fact that I’m thinking that way at all.\n\nThey’re not constant every minute, but they’ve been coming up enough that I can’t just brush them off as a passing bad mood. It feels more like these quiet thoughts in the background, especially when I’m worn down. I haven’t made any plan, and I haven’t taken steps to do anything to myself, but the thoughts are there, and that’s not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow more days than not over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day in some dramatic way, but more days than not, yes, the thought has been there.\n\nIt’s often not loud, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. That’s the part that really scared me enough to come in, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say something like that.\n\nI don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with this grayness and lack of pleasure, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not more than two years. It feels long, and I suppose in the middle of it eight months can feel like forever, but it hasn’t been years.\n\nIt really started about eight months ago, around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That was when I began noticing this grayness settling in, and things I used to enjoy just didn’t feel the same. Before that, I had difficult days, of course, but not this same ongoing lack of pleasure. This has been more like a stretch of months since all of that happened with him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. Maybe not every single thing, but the things that used to feel like mine, like they gave me a little bit of quiet joy, don’t really do that now.\n\nWatercolor is the clearest example. I still set my things out sometimes — the paper, the brushes, the little pans of color — almost like if I go through the motions, maybe the feeling will come back. I’ll sit there looking at the blank paper, and I can remember that I used to feel something when I saw the colors and the light, especially the way water would move the pigment around. It used to be calming, almost absorbing.\n\nBut now I just sit there. I stare at it, and nothing really arrives. No spark, no pleasure in it. After a while I end up putting it all away again, and that feels awful in its own quiet way, because it’s not that I don’t know how to do it anymore. It’s just that the part of me that used to enjoy it feels sort of… unreachable.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower or faster than usual over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself moving faster, no — I don’t think I’ve been pacing or feeling physically wound up like that.\n\nSlower is harder to answer. I feel slower inside, if that makes sense, like it takes more effort to get myself going, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m actually moving slowly. No one has told me that I’m talking slower or walking oddly or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a big change in my actual movements. It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  {

I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really measured it, if that makes sense.

I do feel worn down a lot, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a clear decrease in energy, like my body has less fuel than it used to. Some days I move through things more heavily, and I sit longer before starting something, but I don’t know if that’s energy exactly or just… not wanting to begin. It’s hard for me to separate those.

I’m not collapsing into bed all day or anything like that. I can still get up and do the basic things I need to do. It just often feels like there’s more resistance before I do them. So I suppose I’ve noticed feeling kind of drained at times, but I’m not completely sure whether that’s the kind of decrease in energy you mean.

After a while I just pack it all away again. It’s not that I hate it now. At least, not in a way I can clearly separate out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not very much, no. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to sound as though everything is completely flat, but… the things I used to look forward to don’t seem to pull me toward them the way they did.\n\nThe clearest one is still the watercolor painting. I’ll sometimes get everything out—the paints, the brushes, the little jar of water, the paper taped down just so—and there’s a part of me that remembers liking all of it. I remember how I used to enjoy noticing the color in a flower, or the way light sat on the edge of a table. But now I’ll sit there looking at the paper, and I’m almost waiting for that feeling to arrive, and it just… doesn’t. After a while it starts to feel a bit silly, sitting there like that, so I pack it all away again.\n\nSo I suppose I still try to do the activities, or at least I circle around them, but I can’t honestly say I look forward to them or get much pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasurable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, even a little bit, but it’s hard to find an honest example. The closest thing is still my watercolors, because I do sometimes set everything out as though I’m going to paint. I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I look at the paper, and I look at the colors, and I can remember that I used to love that—the way one color would soften into another, or the way light would catch on something ordinary. I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. That’s the part that really scared me enough to come in, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say something like that.\n\nI don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with this grayness and lack of pleasure, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not more than two years. Since then, those thoughts have just… appeared now and then, especially when I’m alone with it. Most days, really, it feels like there’s this gray film over everything. That sounds a bit poetic, maybe, but it’s the closest way I can describe it. I’ll be doing something ordinary, like making a cup of tea, or standing at the window looking out at my garden, and it’s not that anything terrible is happening in that moment, exactly. It’s just… there’s this quiet heaviness sitting in me.\n\nAnd I try to talk myself out of it sometimes. I’ll think, “Well, the garden is lovely,” or “You have no reason to feel this way right now,” but reasoning with it doesn’t seem to lift it. It’s just there, most days, like the color has been turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, unfortunately. I used to paint a bit—watercolors, mostly. Nothing grand, just small things, flowers from the garden or the way the light fell across the table in the afternoon. It used to give me this very quiet pleasure, choosing the colors, watching them bleed into each other on the paper.\n\nAnd I still get the things out sometimes. I’ll lay out the paints and fill the little jar with water and tape down the paper, almost as if going through the motions might remind me how it used to feel. But then I just sit there looking at the blank page. I can see that there’s color in front of me, and light, and all the things I used to love noticing… but the feeling doesn’t come. After a while I just pack it all away again. It’s not that I hate it now. It’s more that the pleasure seems to have gone missing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worry or anxiety over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean… I do worry, of course, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things over too much, so it’s hard for me to know where ordinary worry ends and something else begins.\n\nOver these months, I suppose there have been moments when my mind gets caught on things and turns them over and over, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a separate feeling of being anxious in the way people describe it—like panic, or being keyed up all the time. It’s more… I don’t know. More of a heaviness than a nervousness, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say worry has been the main thing I’ve noticed. At least, not in a way I can clearly separate out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not very much, no. I keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to sound as though everything is completely flat, but… the things I used to look forward to don’t seem to pull me toward them the way they did.\n\nThe clearest one is still the watercolor painting. I’ll sometimes get everything out—the paints, the brushes, the little jar of water, the paper taped down just so—and there’s a part of me that remembers liking all of it. I remember how I used to enjoy noticing the color in a flower, or the way light sat on the edge of a table. But now I’ll sit there looking at the paper, and I’m almost waiting for that feeling to arrive, and it just… doesn’t. After a while it starts to feel a bit silly, sitting there like that, so I pack it all away again.\n\nSo I suppose I still try to do the activities, or at least I circle around them, but I can’t honestly say I look forward to them or get much pleasure from them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasurable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, even a little bit, but it’s hard to find an honest example. The closest thing is still my watercolors, because I do sometimes set everything out as though I’m going to paint. I’ll put the brushes in order, get the water, choose the paper, and for a moment it almost looks like something I might enjoy.\n\nBut then I just sit there. I look at the paper, and I look at the colors, and I can remember that I used to love that—the way one color would soften into another, or the way light would catch on something ordinary. I remember enjoying it, but I don’t really feel it. It’s like I’m waiting for the pleasure to switch on, and it just doesn’t.\n\nAfter a while I usually feel a bit defeated and put it all away again. So… no, not really. I still go near the things I used to enjoy, but the enjoyment itself doesn’t seem to arrive.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to check myself on that, because I don’t want to be unfair and say there’s absolutely nothing, but when I think about what I actually look forward to… I can’t really find much.\n\nWith the watercolors, for example, I still go through the motions sometimes. I’ll take everything out and set it up properly—the paper, the brushes, the paints, the little cup of water—and there’s almost this memory in my hands of how it used to feel. I used to look forward to that. I’d notice a bit of color somewhere or the way light fell across something, and I’d think, oh, I want to try to paint that.\n\nBut now I’ll sit there in front of the blank paper, and I’m sort of waiting for that feeling to come back. And it doesn’t. The colors are there, the light is there, all the same things are there, but whatever pleasure I used to get from it just doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So I suppose I still try to approach it, but I don’t really look forward to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, I’ve been trying not to say it too dramatically, because I do still make little attempts. It isn’t that I never touch the things at all. With the watercolors, I still sometimes take everything out and set it up—the paints, the brushes, the paper, the water—and I almost expect something to happen because it used to be such a real pleasure for me.\n\nBut then I’ll just sit there staring at the paper. I can remember loving the colors, and the light, and the way the paint would move, but the feeling doesn’t really come. It’s like I know what I’m supposed to enjoy, but I can’t actually reach it. After a while I usually just put everything away again. So yes, over these past months, it does feel like I’ve lost interest in something that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping close track of my weight. I don’t weigh myself regularly, and I don’t think there’s been anything dramatic enough that I’ve noticed it in my clothes or had someone comment on it.\n\nThere may have been small changes here and there, but nothing I could confidently call significant over the past eight months. I suppose if you needed an exact answer, I’d have to check, because I just don’t really have a number in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you move or react to things over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything very clear that way. I mean, I do feel like I move through things differently sometimes, but I couldn’t say, “Oh yes, I’m definitely much slower,” or “I’m reacting too quickly.” It’s not like someone has pointed it out to me, and I haven’t been keeping track of it in any formal way.\n\nThere are moments when I probably take longer to get going, but I don’t know whether that’s the kind of thing you mean—like physically moving more slowly, or taking longer to answer people, or just feeling a bit stuck. I suppose if you watched me closely maybe you’d see something, but from inside it, I can’t really tell. Nothing stands out to me as a major change over the past eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been unable to experience pleasure from almost all activities most of the time over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say almost all activities, because that sounds so absolute, and I’m not sure I can measure it that neatly. But with the things that used to genuinely give me pleasure, yes, there’s been a real flattening.\n\nThe clearest example is the watercolors. I still set everything out sometimes, almost out of habit or hope—the paints, the brushes, the little cup of water, the paper. And I’ll sit there thinking, well, surely once I start, that old feeling will come back. I used to love the color and the light, the way the paint would spread and blend. It wasn’t just a hobby; it was something I could really lose myself in.\n\nBut lately I just stare at the paper. I can remember that I used to enjoy it, but the pleasure itself doesn’t seem to arrive. After a while it feels sort of pointless, and I put everything away again. So yes, most of the time, at least with that kind of thing, it feels like I’m going through the motions without actually getting the enjoyment from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts of death or dying frequently over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually part of what scared me enough to come in.\n\nIt isn’t that I have a plan to hurt myself, because I don’t. I want to be clear about that. But I’ve found myself having thoughts like, “It would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow,” or that if something happened naturally, I wouldn’t have to keep trying so hard. And then I get frightened by the fact that I’m thinking that way at all.\n\nThey’re not constant every minute, but they’ve been coming up enough that I can’t just brush them off as a passing bad mood. It feels more like these quiet thoughts in the background, especially when I’m worn down. I haven’t made any plan, and I haven’t taken steps to do anything to myself, but the thoughts are there, and that’s not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having these thoughts of not waking up tomorrow more days than not over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day in some dramatic way, but more days than not, yes, the thought has been there.\n\nIt’s often not loud, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’ll wake up or go to bed and have this feeling of, oh, I have to do all of this again, and then the thought comes in that it would be simpler if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow. That’s the part that really scared me enough to come in, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say something like that.\n\nI don’t have a plan to harm myself. I haven’t been making plans or anything like that. But the thought itself has been showing up often enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way, with this grayness and lack of pleasure, for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not more than two years. It feels long, and I suppose in the middle of it eight months can feel like forever, but it hasn’t been years.\n\nIt really started about eight months ago, around when my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly. That was when I began noticing this grayness settling in, and things I used to enjoy just didn’t feel the same. Before that, I had difficult days, of course, but not this same ongoing lack of pleasure. This has been more like a stretch of months since all of that happened with him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. Maybe not every single thing, but the things that used to feel like mine, like they gave me a little bit of quiet joy, don’t really do that now.\n\nWatercolor is the clearest example. I still set my things out sometimes — the paper, the brushes, the little pans of color — almost like if I go through the motions, maybe the feeling will come back. I’ll sit there looking at the blank paper, and I can remember that I used to feel something when I saw the colors and the light, especially the way water would move the pigment around. It used to be calming, almost absorbing.\n\nBut now I just sit there. I stare at it, and nothing really arrives. No spark, no pleasure in it. After a while I end up putting it all away again, and that feels awful in its own quiet way, because it’s not that I don’t know how to do it anymore. It’s just that the part of me that used to enjoy it feels sort of… unreachable.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower or faster than usual over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself moving faster, no — I don’t think I’ve been pacing or feeling physically wound up like that.\n\nSlower is harder to answer. I feel slower inside, if that makes sense, like it takes more effort to get myself going, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m actually moving slowly. No one has told me that I’m talking slower or walking oddly or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t clearly noticed a big change in my actual movements. It’s more that everything feels like it takes more effort, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in energy over the past eight months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t really measured it, if that makes sense.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a clear decrease in energy, like my body has less fuel than it used to. Some days I move through things more heavily, and I sit longer before starting something, but I don’t know if that’s energy exactly or just… not wanting to begin. It’s hard for me to separate those.\n\nI’m not collapsing into bed all day or anything like that. I can still get up and do the basic things I need to do. It just often feels like there’s more resistance before I do them. So I suppose I’ve noticed feeling kind of drained at times, but I’m not completely sure whether that’s the kind of decrease in energy you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
